[^1871]: Martyrikon troparion concluding the Thursday Matins Aposticha.
[^1872]: The ancient Marian prayer *All my hope do I place upon thee* (*Vse upovanïye moye na tya vozlagayu*), chanted at the close of the Matins Aposticha.
[^1873]: Liturgical rubric for the conclusion of Thursday Matins: Psalm 91:2 ("It is good to give praise unto the Lord"), Trisagion prayers, Our Father, daily dismissory troparia, and the Litany of Fervent Supplication.
[^1874]: First Beatitude troparion of Thursday Liturgy, invoking the Good Thief's cry from the cross (Luke 23:42). The printed text contains an epigraphic diminutive form or typographical error: *razboynichk'* (разбо́йничкь) for *razboynich'* (разбо́йничь).
[^1875]: Second Beatitude troparion, celebrating the universal mission of the Apostles delivering mankind from demonic darkness.
[^1876]: Third Beatitude troparion, employing the evangelical metaphor of the Apostles as fishers of men (Matthew 4:19) drawing souls from the sea of worldly vanity.
[^1877]: Fourth Beatitude troparion, commemorating the holy martyrs who finished the course and kept the faith (2 Timothy 4:7).
[^1878]: Doxasticon / Triadikon of the Beatitudes, confessing the uncreated Trinity and the co-equal divine operation (*homoenergeia*) of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.
[^1879]: Concluding Marian Beatitude troparion, praising the Mother of God as the boast of the Apostles and crown of the martyrs.
[^1880]: Liturgical opening of the Friday cycle in Tone 2, dedicated to the Precious and Life-Giving Cross and the Passion of Christ, beginning on Thursday evening at Vespers.
[^1881]: First Sticheron of the Cross at Thursday Vespers, commemorating the cosmic upheavals attending the Crucifixion of Christ (Matthew 27:51–52; Luke 23:44–45).
[^1882]: Second Sticheron of the Cross, celebrating the overthrow of Satan and the liberation of Adam from the ancestral curse through Christ's elevation upon the Cross.
[^1883]: Third Sticheron of the Cross, depicting the harrowing of hades and the jubilation of the forefather Adam at Christ's voluntary passion.
[^1884]: Rubrical directive for the remaining Stichera on "Lord, I have cried": appointed from the Menaion, or alternatively from the supplementary Stavrotheotokia in Tone 2.
[^1885]: First Stavrotheotokion, the maternal lamentation of the Mother of God standing before the Cross of her Son.
[^1886]: Second Stavrotheotokion, recounting the piercing of Christ's side with a lance (John 19:34) and the anguish of the Theotokos.
[^1887]: Third Stavrotheotokion, depicting the Pieta / Deposition from the Cross, the Virgin embracing and kissing the dead body of Christ before burial.
[^1888]: Doxasticon Stavrotheotokion at the Stichera on "Lord, I have cried," portraying the Mother of God as the spotless Ewe-lamb beholding the Lamb of God led to slaughter (Isaiah 53:7).
[^1889]: Rubric for the standard evening order: the vesperal hymn *Phos Hilaron*, the Thursday evening daily prokeimenon ("My help comes from the Lord," Psalm 120:2), and the evening prayer *Kataxioson* ("Vouchsafe, O Lord").
[^1890]: First Apostichon of the Cross at Thursday Vespers, linking the saving power of the Cross with Christ stretching forth His hand to save the sinking Peter on the sea (Matthew 14:30–31).
[^1891]: Psalm 122:1, 2b [LXX 122:1, 2 / MT 123:1, 2], the standard first verse of the daily Vespers Aposticha.
[^1892]: Second Apostichon of the Cross, contrasting Christ's divine gifts with the crowd's demand for Barabbas and the condemnation of Jesus (Matthew 27:20–23).
[^1893]: Psalm 122:3a, 4 [LXX 122:3, 4 / MT 123:3, 4], the standard second verse of the daily Vespers Aposticha.
[^1894]: Martyrikon troparion concluding the Vespers Aposticha in Tone 2.
[^1895]: Doxasticon Stavrotheotokion at the Aposticha, meditating on the cosmic paradox of Christ hanging between thieves, He who suspended the earth upon the waters (Job 26:7).
[^1896]: Concluding rubrics for Thursday Vespers: the Canticle of Simeon (Luke 2:29–32), Trisagion prayers, Our Father, the Troparion of the Cross ("Save, O Lord, Thy people"), and the standard dismissal.
[^1897]: Ode 1 heirmos of the Supplicatory Canon to the Theotokos at Small Compline, Tone 2, drawing upon the Song of the Sea (Exodus 15:1).
[^1898]: Opening of Troparion 1 of Ode 1 (Compline Canon), praising the unmatched beauty and purity of the Virgin (Song of Songs 4:7), continued on leaf p224.
[^1899]: Conclusion of Troparion 1 of Ode 1, continued from leaf p223.
[^1900]: Troparion of Ode 1, quoting Psalm 44:10 [LXX 44:10 / MT 45:9]: "At Thy right hand stood the queen in a vesture of gold," praying for a place at Christ's right hand at the Last Judgment.
[^1901]: Doxasticon troparion of Ode 1, applying the metaphor of the parched furrow made fruitful by the Heavenly Rain of Christ's grace.
[^1902]: Concluding Marian troparion of Ode 1, contrasting the tree of the knowledge of good and evil (Genesis 2:17) with the Tree of Life, Christ incarnate of the Virgin.
[^1903]: Ode 3 heirmos of the Compline canon, drawn from Isaiah 35:1 and 1 Kingdoms 2:1 [1 Samuel 2:1].
[^1904]: First troparion of Ode 3, commemorating the Incarnation wherein the Creator assumes human nature to clothe Adam, stripped by sin, in the robe of incorruption (Genesis 3:21; Galatians 3:27).
[^1905]: Second troparion of Ode 3, beseeching God the Word to show mercy upon the soul grieving over sinful passions.
[^1906]: Doxasticon troparion of Ode 3, imploring the Theotokos to administer divine medicine against the venom of the ancestral serpent.
[^1907]: Concluding Marian troparion of Ode 3, pleading for maternal intercession to defend the faithful and overthrow the arrogance of the impious.
[^1908]: Ode 4 heirmos of the Compline canon, drawn from the Canticle of Habakkuk (Habakkuk 3:2–3) and Isaiah 63:9.
[^1909]: First troparion of Ode 4, praying for tears of compunction (*katanyxis*) to extinguish the burning inflammation of passions.
[^1910]: Opening of Troparion 2 of Ode 4, confessing the wounds inflicted by sinful pleasures, continued on leaf p225.
[^1911]: Conclusion of Troparion 2 of Ode 4, continued from leaf p224, appealing to the healing flow from the pierced side of the Crucified Lord (John 19:34).
[^1912]: Doxasticon troparion of Ode 4, supplicating the Mother of God, who possesses the riches of grace, to enrich the spiritually destitute soul.
[^1913]: Concluding Marian troparion of Ode 4, identifying Christ as the Father's Radiance (Hebrews 1:3) dispelling demonic darkness through His Crucifixion.
[^1914]: Ode 5 heirmos of the Compline canon, drawn from 1 Timothy 2:5 and Isaiah 26:9.
[^1915]: First troparion of Ode 5, confessing Christ as the Way (John 14:6) and pleading for deliverance from the precipice of sin.
[^1916]: Second troparion of Ode 5, alluding to the Parable of the Prodigal Son wasting his substance in a far country (Luke 15:13).
[^1917]: Doxasticon troparion of Ode 5, invoking the Virgin as a channel of living waters (John 7:38) quenching the flames of passions.
[^1918]: Concluding Marian troparion of Ode 5, citing the messianic prophecy of Isaiah (Isaiah 7:14). The 1895 Lviv print exhibits a typographical error: *i mela yesi* (и҆ мѣ́ла є҆сѝ) for *imela yesi* (имѣ́ла є҆сѝ).
[^1919]: Ode 6 heirmos of the Compline canon, drawn from the Canticle of Jonah (Jonah 2:3, 7) and Psalm 129:1 [LXX 129:1 / MT 130:1].
[^1920]: First troparion of Ode 6, begging that the soul be not surrendered to the mocking joy of demons at the Last Judgment.
[^1921]: Second troparion of Ode 6, presenting the Mother of God as an all-efficacious intercessor to appease the divine wrath provoked by sinful thoughts.
[^1922]: Doxasticon troparion of Ode 6, addressing the Theotokos as the Mother of the Supreme Judge.
[^1923]: Concluding Marian troparion of Ode 6, beseeching the Virgin Mother to intercede with Jesus the Savior for deliverance from tribulations.
[^1924]: Rubric directing the threefold *Kyrie Eleison*, *Doxa Patri*, and the chanting of the Sessional Hymn after Ode 6.
[^1925]: Stavrotheotokion Sessional Hymn following Ode 6 at Compline, contemplating the Mother of God's sorrow before the mystery of the voluntary Crucifixion.
[^1926]: Ode 7 heirmos of the Compline canon, based on the furnace of Babylon (Daniel 3:19–28).
[^1927]: First troparion of Ode 7, drawing upon Psalm 117:14 [LXX 117:14 / MT 118:14] ("The Lord is my strength and my song, and He is become my salvation").
[^1928]: Second troparion of Ode 7, confessing the deification (*theosis*) of human nature through the Incarnation of God from the virginal blood of Mary.
[^1929]: Doxasticon troparion of Ode 7, interpreting the unburning furnace of Babylon as a type of Mary's womb bearing the consuming fire of the Godhead unburnt.
[^1930]: Concluding Marian troparion of Ode 7, confessing the perpetual virginity of the Theotokos (*aeiparthenia*) before, during, and after childbearing.
[^1931]: Ode 8 heirmos of the Compline canon, based on Daniel 3:57.
[^1932]: First troparion of Ode 8, exhorting the soul to virtues under the patronage of the Mother of God.

[^1937]: Ode 9 heirmos of the Compline canon, drawn from Luke 1:78–79 and John 11:52.
[^1938]: First troparion of Ode 9, contrasting the bitter taste from the tree of knowledge with the sweetness of immortality flowing from the Tree of the Cross.
[^1939]: Second troparion of Ode 9, addressing Mary as Queen and Mother of the King Who destroyed the realm of death.
[^1940]: Doxasticon troparion of Ode 9, pleading for entrance through the gates of repentance.
[^1941]: Concluding troparion of Ode 9, meditating on Christ's death upon the Cross putting the ancient serpent to death and reviving the deadened soul.
[^1942]: Rubrical conclusion of Small Compline: the Marian megalynarion *Axion Estin*, Trisagion prayers, Our Father, and dismissal troparia.

[^1983]: Heirmos of Ode 4, referencing the Christological citation of Isaiah 63:9 (LXX: "neither an ambassador, nor an angel, but He Himself saved them"). Note "therefore" used in place of pseudo-archaic "therefore".

[^1985]: Second troparion of Ode 4, presenting the Passion of Christ as the restorative reversal of the Fall of Adam and Eve.

[^1987]: Second Martyrikon troparion of Ode 4, recounting the tortures of the martyrs—crucifixion, scourging, and flaying—as a total defeat of the demonic enemy.
[^1988]: Stavrotheotokion of Ode 4, depicting the weeping of the Theotokos as she contemplates the injustice of Christ's execution and His boundless loving-kindness.

[^2009]: First Martyrikon troparion of Ode 6, praising the martyrs who rejected the counsel of the ungodly (Psalm 1:1) to gain eternal life.

[^2011]: Stavrotheotokion of Ode 6, stating the classic patristic theosis soteriology: God became man and suffered crucifixion in order to deify mankind and slay the ancient slayer.

[^2019]: Second troparion of Ode 7, referencing Colossians 2:14 (nailing the handwriting of ordinances to the Cross) and the mocking reed (Matthew 27:29) transformed into the pen of manumission.
[^2020]: First Martyrikon troparion of Ode 7, describing how the bodily dismemberment of the martyrs was met with unbroken spiritual union with God and victory over the adversary.

[^2071]: First sticheron at "Lord, I have cried" on Friday evening, chanted twice, celebrating the martyrs' victory over idol worship and standing before the throne of God.
[^2072]: Second sticheron at "Lord, I have cried," honoring the Holy Hierarchs and Venerable Doctors who formulated Orthodox dogma and expelled heresy from the Church.
[^2073]: Idiomelon/Martyrikon stichera at "Lord, I have cried" in Tone 2.
[^2074]: First Martyrikon sticheron, contrasting transient earthly pleasure with celestial bliss.
[^2075]: Second Martyrikon sticheron, exalting the intercession of the martyrs in overthrowing demonic deception.
[^2076]: Third Martyrikon sticheron, dramatizing the bold confession of the martyrs before pagan judges.
[^2077]: Tone 2 Friday evening Dogmatikon Theotokion, masterfully setting forth Old Testament typological antitypes: the shadow of the law yielding to grace (Hebrews 10:1), the unburnt bush foreshadowing virginity in childbearing (Exodus 3:2), the pillar of fire yielding to the Sun of Righteousness (Malachi 4:2), and Moses yielding to Christ (John 1:17).
[^2078]: Vespers rubrics: the ancient hymn Phos Hilaron (*O Joyful Light*), the Friday evening Prokeimenon (Psalm 58:10, 18 [LXX]), and the evening prayer *Kataxioson* (*Vouchsafe, O Lord*).

[^2080]: First Aposticha sticheron, venerating the healing and exorcistic power of the relics of the holy martyrs.
[^2081]: Psalm 64:5 [LXX], the canonical verse for the commemoration of the departed.
[^2082]: Hymn for the Departed (Поко́инъ) sticheron for the departed, contrasting earthly vanity (Job 14:2; Psalm 102:15 [LXX]) with the universal resurrection at the last trumpet (1 Corinthians 15:52; 1 Thessalonians 4:16-17).
[^2083]: Psalm 24:13 [LXX].
